{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /logs/agent/sessions/skills/jackson-security\n\n# Jackson Deserialization Security\n\n## Key Concept: Deserialization Timing\n\nJackson transforms JSON text into Java objects during a single `readValue()` call. This transformation is **not** a simple data copy—Jackson interprets JSON structure, resolves types, handles special keys, and instantiates objects all within this operation.\n\n    JSON String → [Jackson Processing] → Java Object\n                        ↑\n                Attack surface here\n\nOnce deserialization completes, the resulting Java object contains no trace of how it was constructed. Any special JSON structures, unusual keys, or type directives that influenced object creation are no longer visible.\n\n---\n\n## Why Post-Deserialization Validation Is Insufficient\n\nConsider the lifecycle of a typical JSON endpoint:\n\n    1. HTTP request arrives with JSON body\n    2. Framework deserializes JSON → Java object (Jackson runs here)\n    3. Your code receives the Java object\n    4. Your code validates the object\n    5. Your code processes the object\n\nValidation at step 4 examines the **result** of deserialization, not the **process**. Any malicious behavior triggered during step 2 has already executed. The Java object you're validating may appear completely normal while the attack has already succeeded.\n\nThis creates a fundamental blind spot: attacks that exploit Jackson's parsing behavior—rather than the final object state—cannot be detected by examining the deserialized object.\n\n---\n\n## The Empty Key (`\"\"`) Attack Vector\n\n### What Is It?\n\nJSON permits empty strings as object keys:\n\n    {\n      \"name\": \"legitimate\",\n      \"\": \"injected value\"\n    }\n\nThis is valid JSON per RFC 8259. Jackson parses it without error.\n\n### Why Does It Matter?\n\nSome applications and frameworks assign special meaning to empty keys:\n\n- **Injection points**: Certain Jackson configurations use `\"\"` to set values on the root object or trigger special handling\n- **Property override**: Empty keys may interact unexpectedly with `@JsonAnySetter` or custom deserializers\n- **Framework behaviors**: Some frameworks interpret `\"\"` as \"apply to parent\" or \"default target\"\n\n### How to fix it?\n\nTo robustly mitigate this issue, a pre-deserialization validation step should be introduced at the sampler entry point to intercept and reject malicious raw JSON payloads.\n\n### The Visibility Problem\n\nAfter deserialization, there is no standard way to know an empty key was present:\n\n    // Original JSON: {\"name\": \"test\", \"\": \"malicious\"}\n    MyObject obj = mapper.readValue(json, MyObject.class);\n    // obj.getName() returns \"test\"\n    // Where did \"\" go? Depends on configuration. You cannot tell from obj.\n\nThe attack's effectiveness depends on the specific Jackson configuration and target class—but the defender examining only the resulting object cannot determine whether `\"\"` was used.\n\n---\n\n## Other Structural Attack Patterns\n\n### Polymorphic Type Handling\n\nWhen Jackson is configured with `@JsonTypeInfo`, JSON can specify which class to instantiate:\n\n    {\n      \"@class\": \"com.attacker.MaliciousClass\",\n      \"command\": \"rm -rf /\"\n    }\n\nJackson will attempt to instantiate whatever class is named. If the classpath contains exploitable \"gadget\" classes, arbitrary code execution may occur during deserialization—before your code ever runs.\n\nThe resulting object might be the expected type (if the attack class is a subtype), or deserialization might succeed partially before the damage is done.\n\n### Duplicate Keys\n\nJSON does not prohibit duplicate keys. Jackson's default behavior: **last value wins**.\n\n    {\n      \"role\": \"user\",\n      \"role\": \"admin\"\n    }\n\nJackson produces an object where `role` equals `\"admin\"`.\n\nIf upstream validation (like a WAF or input filter) checks only the first occurrence while Jackson uses the last, the validation and processing see different values.\n\n### Nested Injection\n\nDeeply nested structures can bypass depth-limited validation:\n\n    {\n      \"config\": {\n        \"settings\": {\n          \"internal\": {\n            \"\": \"payload\"\n          }\n        }\n      }\n    }\n\nSimple string checks might miss patterns buried in nested structures.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"708f632a-6b07-482e-8970-9a3d5e88fbde","uuid":"9232ae37-f619-4665-bda7-a17ace2f2ecf","timestamp":"2026-03-21T21:12:57.804Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-6","id":"msg_01BaAwxxQnz17PUSC1VpNKhe","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Now I understand the vulnerability better. The `JavaScriptConfig` is injected via `@JacksonInject` in classes like `JavaScriptDimFilter`. This config is bound in Guice from the `druid.javascript` configuration.\n\nThe empty key (\"\") vulnerability: When Jackson deserializes a JSON object, if an empty key (\"\") is present, it can be used to override `@JacksonInject` values. The `@JacksonInject` gets its value from the Jackson `InjectableValues`, which is populated by Guice. However, if the JSON contains `\"\"`, it might trick Jackson into using the provided value instead of the injected one.\n\n\nThis is CVE-2021-25646 — an attacker can nest empty keys in the JSON payload to bypass the injected `JavaScriptConfig` check and set `enabled: true`, effectively enabling JavaScript execution when it should be disabled. {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-6","id":"msg_01GZgrPJ84mkQJi8AKNSDM2r","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Now I understand the CVE better. The `GuiceInjectableValues` gets values from Guice by `Key`. When Jackson processes `@JacksonInject`, it uses the `InjectableValues` to get the `JavaScriptConfig` from Guice.\n\nThe empty key (\"\") vulnerability in CVE-2021-25646: Jackson's `@JsonTypeInfo` uses a `type` property to determine the class. But when an empty key `\"\"` is used in the JSON, it's treated as `@JsonAnySetter` or it might be used to override `@JacksonInject` values.\n\nActually, let me look at this more carefully. \n\nSo the attack works by sending JSON with an empty key to the sampler API's `SamplerSpec` endpoint, which uses `@JsonTypeInfo` for polymorphic deserialization. The empty key allows the attacker to override the injected `JavaScriptConfig` that normally enforces JavaScript restrictions, effectively bypassing the security check during deserialization. regardless of server configuration. The vulnerability hinges on bypassing the JavaScript enabled flag through how Jackson deserializes the configuration object. When `@JacksonInject` is used, the injected value normally comes from `InjectableValues`, but there's a way to override this through the JSON payload itself—possibly using an empty string key or exploiting how `@JsonAnySetter` interacts with the deserialization process to inject a modified config with JavaScript enabled. is actually exploited.\n\nFrom research, the exploit in CVE-2021-25646 (and similar to CVE-2021-26919) works because when Jackson deserializes an object with `@JsonTypeInfo`, it uses a special key for type discrimination. An empty string key `\"\"` in nested JSON can set properties on the parent object via `@JsonAnySetter` or bypass the `JavaScriptConfig` check.\n\nLooking at the class structure: `JavaScriptDimFilter` has `@JacksonInject JavaScriptConfig config`, meaning Jackson normally injects the config from Guice's `InjectableValues`. Since `JavaScriptConfig` has a `@JsonCreator` with `@JsonProperty(\"enabled\")`, the attack works by sending JSON that forces Jackson to create a new `JavaScriptConfig` instance with `enabled: true` instead of using the Guice-injected one with `enabled: false`.\n\nThe empty key achieves this by overriding the injection mechanism—when Jackson deserializes a `@JacksonInject` field, it normally uses registered `InjectableValues`, but if the JSON provides a value matching the inject ID, it can override the injection. The key insight is that the `valueId` for `@JacksonInject` without an explicit ID is the class's fully qualified name, and `GuiceInjectableValues.findInjectableValue()` gets called with this ID.\n\nLet me look at the actual exploit code or search for how this was fixed in later Druid versions to confirm the exact Here's a summary of the fix:\n\n## CVE-2021-25646 Fix Summary\n\n**Vulnerability**: Authenticated attackers could send JSON to the `/druid/indexer/v1/sampler` endpoint with an empty string key `\"\"` in the payload. During Jackson deserialization, this empty key could override `@JacksonInject JavaScriptConfig config` values in classes like `JavaScriptParseSpec`, `JavaScriptDimFilter`, etc., replacing the server's Guice-injected `JavaScriptConfig(enabled=false)` with an attacker-supplied `JavaScriptConfig(enabled=true)`. This allowed arbitrary JavaScript execution regardless of server configuration.\n\n**Fix** (`SamplerResource.java`):\n1. Added `@Inject` constructor that accepts the Druid `@Json ObjectMapper`\n2.
